People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Maybe not moderate exactly… I mean, I’m still doing my job and getting things done. But I guess there have been some effects. Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, kind of empty feeling in my chest. I don’t want to scare anyone, and I’m not planning to hurt myself. Maybe a little, but I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I mean… I still do the things I need to do, and if someone invites me or it’s part of my routine, I’ll usually go along with it. And at home my wife has kind of taken over some of the bills and scheduling for the same reason.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that’s “moderate” or still mild, but it’s more than nothing.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current level of impairment as significant enough to interfere substantially with your social or occupational functioning?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s substantial, exactly… at least I hope it doesn’t come across that way. Does that answer it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your feelings of sadness are mild or they interfere with your daily life more significantly?\n\nPatient: I’d say… mild, I guess, in the sense that I’m still getting through the day. I can go to my shift and talk to people and do what I’m supposed to do. But lately I do wake up with this heavy, kind of empty feeling in my chest, and inside I feel sad and flat, even if I’m functioning on the outside. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say these sad and flat feelings are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day… I think so, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I’m crying all day or unable to do anything. I can still get through my shift and talk to people. But that heavy, empty feeling is kind of there from when I wake up, and underneath everything I feel sad and flat. I don’t know if that counts as “most of the day,” but it feels like it’s around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have your sad and flat feelings been present for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s right around two weeks. This whole shift kind of started after my uncle died, about two weeks ago. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but that’s when I noticed I started feeling more sad and flat. Is that the kind of timing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sad and flat feelings are interfering with your daily life in a way that you would describe as moderate?\n\nPatient: Maybe not moderate exactly… I mean, I’m still doing my job and getting things done. But I guess there have been some effects. At work I’ve asked a couple coworkers to double-check my client documentation, just because I don’t feel as reliable with the details right now. And at home my wife has kind of taken over some of the bills and scheduling for the same reason.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that’s “moderate” or still mild, but it’s more than nothing.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current level of impairment as significant enough to interfere substantially with your social or occupational functioning?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s substantial, exactly… at least I hope it doesn’t come across that way. I’m still showing up and doing what I need to do.\n\nBut there is some interference. At work I’ve had to ask coworkers to double-check my client documentation, and at home my wife has taken over some of the bills and scheduling because I’m not as reliable with details right now. So it’s affecting things, but I’d probably describe it as mild rather than a major impairment, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current symptoms are causing you more than minor difficulties in your social or occupational functioning?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little more than minor, but I don’t want to overstate it. I’m still functioning.\n\nIt’s just that at work I’ve had to ask coworkers to double-check my client documentation, because I don’t totally trust myself with the details right now. And at home, my wife has taken over some of the bills and scheduling for the same reason.\n\nSo yeah, it’s causing some difficulty, I guess. I’m not sure if that counts as more than minor to you.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current symptoms are causing significant distress for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I can still get through my shift and talk to people.\n\nBut lately when I wake up, there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m there and functioning, but inside I feel kind of sad and flat. So… yes, it is distressing, I think. I don’t know if I’d call it severe, but it’s definitely there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I can go to my shift and talk to people and do what I’m supposed to do. But lately I do wake up with this heavy, kind of empty feeling in my chest, and inside I feel sad and flat, even if I’m functioning on the outside. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say these sad and flat feelings are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day… I think so, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I’m crying all day or unable to do anything. I can still get through my shift and talk to people. But that heavy, empty feeling is kind of there from when I wake up, and underneath everything I feel sad and flat. I don’t know if that counts as “most of the day,” but it feels like it’s around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have your sad and flat feelings been present for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s right around two weeks. This whole shift kind of started after my uncle died, about two weeks ago. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but that’s when I noticed I started feeling more sad and flat. Is that the kind of timing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sad and flat feelings are interfering with your daily life in a way that you would describe as moderate?\n\nPatient: Maybe not moderate exactly… I mean, I’m still doing my job and getting things done. But I guess there have been some effects. At work I’ve asked a couple coworkers to double-check my client documentation, just because I don’t feel as reliable with the details right now. And at home my wife has kind of taken over some of the bills and scheduling for the same reason.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that’s “moderate” or still mild, but it’s more than nothing.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current level of impairment as significant enough to interfere substantially with your social or occupational functioning?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s substantial, exactly… at least I hope it doesn’t come across that way. I’m still showing up and doing what I need to do.\n\nBut there is some interference. At work I’ve had to ask coworkers to double-check my client documentation, and at home my wife has taken over some of the bills and scheduling because I’m not as reliable with details right now. So it’s affecting things, but I’d probably describe it as mild rather than a major impairment, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current symptoms are causing you more than minor difficulties in your social or occupational functioning?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little more than minor, but I don’t want to overstate it. I’m still functioning.\n\nIt’s just that at work I’ve had to ask coworkers to double-check my client documentation, because I don’t totally trust myself with the details right now. And at home, my wife has taken over some of the bills and scheduling for the same reason.\n\nSo yeah, it’s causing some difficulty, I guess. I’m not sure if that counts as more than minor to you.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current symptoms are causing significant distress for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I can still get through my shift and talk to people.\n\nBut lately when I wake up, there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m there and functioning, but inside I feel kind of sad and flat. So… yes, it is distressing, I think. I don’t know if I’d call it severe, but it’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current depressive symptoms as severely impairing your ability to function?\n\nPatient: Severely… I don’t think I’d call it severe. But that heavy, empty feeling when I wake up — feeling sad and kind of flat inside — that’s been there a lot lately.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy, but now find pleasureless?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. But… yeah, there have been a few mornings where I’ve had this thought like, if I just didn’t wake up, maybe that wouldn’t be the worst thing. And then I feel awful for even thinking that. Is that… is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I still do the usual things if they’re in front of me, but I guess I haven’t really been paying attention to whether I’m enjoying them less. Maybe a little, but I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. What would count as a change?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant loss of interest or pleasure in most of your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been significant, no. I mean… I still do the things I need to do, and if someone invites me or it’s part of my routine, I’ll usually go along with it. Maybe I’m not as excited about stuff lately, but I wouldn’t want to say I’ve lost interest in most things. Does that answer it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your feelings of sadness are mild or they interfere with your daily life more significantly?\n\nPatient: I’d say… mild, I guess, in the sense that I’m still getting through the day. I can go to my shift and talk to people and do what I’m supposed to do. But lately I do wake up with this heavy, kind of empty feeling in my chest, and inside I feel sad and flat, even if I’m functioning on the outside. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say these sad and flat feelings are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day… I think so, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I’m crying all day or unable to do anything. I can still get through my shift and talk to people. But that heavy, empty feeling is kind of there from when I wake up, and underneath everything I feel sad and flat. I don’t know if that counts as “most of the day,” but it feels like it’s around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have your sad and flat feelings been present for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s right around two weeks. This whole shift kind of started after my uncle died, about two weeks ago. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but that’s when I noticed I started feeling more sad and flat. Is that the kind of timing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sad and flat feelings are interfering with your daily life in a way that you would describe as moderate?\n\nPatient: Maybe not moderate exactly… I mean, I’m still doing my job and getting things done. But I guess there have been some effects. At work I’ve asked a couple coworkers to double-check my client documentation, just because I don’t feel as reliable with the details right now. And at home my wife has kind of taken over some of the bills and scheduling for the same reason.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that’s “moderate” or still mild, but it’s more than nothing.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current level of impairment as significant enough to interfere substantially with your social or occupational functioning?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s substantial, exactly… at least I hope it doesn’t come across that way. I’m still showing up and doing what I need to do.\n\nBut there is some interference. At work I’ve had to ask coworkers to double-check my client documentation, and at home my wife has taken over some of the bills and scheduling because I’m not as reliable with details right now. So it’s affecting things, but I’d probably describe it as mild rather than a major impairment, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current symptoms are causing you more than minor difficulties in your social or occupational functioning?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little more than minor, but I don’t want to overstate it. I’m still functioning.\n\nIt’s just that at work I’ve had to ask coworkers to double-check my client documentation, because I don’t totally trust myself with the details right now. And at home, my wife has taken over some of the bills and scheduling for the same reason.\n\nSo yeah, it’s causing some difficulty, I guess. I’m not sure if that counts as more than minor to you.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current symptoms are causing significant distress for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I can still get through my shift and talk to people.\n\nBut lately when I wake up, there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m there and functioning, but inside I feel kind of sad and flat. So… yes, it is distressing, I think. I don’t know if I’d call it severe, but it’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current depressive symptoms as severely impairing your ability to function?\n\nPatient: Severely… I don’t think I’d call it severe. I’m still getting to work and managing, mostly.\n\nBut it is affecting my functioning some. Like I mentioned, I’ve had to ask coworkers to double-check my client documentation because I don’t fully trust myself with the details right now. And at home, my wife’s been handling some of the bills and scheduling because I haven’t been as reliable with that.\n\nSo… impaired, yes, but I’d say more mild than severe, if that sounds right.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past month have you felt this sad and flat?\n\nPatient: Maybe… most days? I don’t want to exaggerate, but I’d say probably around three weeks out of the last month.\n\nIt’s not like I’m crying all day or anything. I still get up and go to work. But that heavy, empty feeling when I wake up — feeling sad and kind of flat inside — that’s been there a lot lately.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy, but now find pleasureless?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I still do some of the same things, and I wouldn’t say nothing feels good anymore.\n\nMaybe some things don’t feel as… satisfying as they used to, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I haven’t really noticed a clear activity where I think, “I used to love this and now it’s completely pleasureless.” Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current symptoms are significantly affecting your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Significantly… maybe not significantly, at least I hope not. My wife and I are okay, and I’m not trying to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut it has affected things some at home. She’s taken over some of the bills and scheduling lately because I haven’t been as reliable with details. And at work I’ve had coworkers double-check my client notes, which isn’t exactly relationships with friends or family, but it does make me feel like I’m leaning on people more than I’d like.\n\nSo… I’d say mildly affecting things, maybe. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current symptoms are causing you significant distress or impairment for most of every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most of every day,” exactly. I’m still functioning, you know, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut it is causing problems. At work I’ve had to ask coworkers to double-check my client documentation, because I’m not as reliable with the details right now. And at home my wife has kind of taken over some of the bills and scheduling for the same reason.\n\nSo… maybe not severe, but it’s definitely impairing things some. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current symptoms are interfering with your daily life to a degree that you would consider moderate or more?\n\nPatient: Moderate or more… I guess I’d say it’s probably on the mild side, but it is interfering.\n\nLike I said, at work I’ve had to ask coworkers to double-check my client notes, because I’m not feeling as reliable with details.
